3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **`docs/paper/discussion.tex`** — Central file for multiple patches. Current full content:
     ```latex
     \section{Discussion}
     \label{sec:discussion}
     \label{sec:why_kstar}
     \label{sec:one_wash}
     \label{sec:negative_graduated}
     \label{sec:repr_dependence}
     \label{sec:boundary_cases}
     \label{sec:length_confound}
     
     \begin{figure}[t]
     \centering
     \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_jsd_decay.pdf}
     \caption{Token-level JSD between adjacent depth pairs on log scale. Blue band: min-max range across 8 C4 models (1B--14B) with $\Kempirical{=}2$; dashed purple: Mistral-7B ($\Kempirical{=}1$, cross-scorer validated); green: arXiv Pythia ($\Kempirical{=}3$); dotted red: retrain-chain ($\Kempirical{\geq}4$). C4 models exhibit a sharp cliff ($\lambda \approx 2.2$--$3.5$), while arXiv decays gradually ($\lambda \approx 1.0$) and retrain-chain shows no cliff ($\lambda = 0.285$).}
     \label{fig:jsd_decay}
     \end{figure}
     
     \paragraph{One-wash laundering (web text).}
     For general web text, $\Kempirical{=}2$ reflects concentrated information loss: 0v1 is near-trivially separable ($\auc \geq 0.92$), 1v2 drops by ${\sim}$30\,pp, and 2v3 falls below $\theta$.
     A single generation pass renders text indistinguishable from multiply recycled content, limiting depth-aware curation to a binary boundary.
     Converging evidence supports a data-intrinsic interpretation: OBD achieves 96\% depth-0 recall but only 14--22\% for depths 2--4; frontier LLMs show 8--38\% (McNemar $p > 0.05$ vs.\ OBD; Table~\ref{tab:s3_llm_zeroshot}).
     Baseline classifier substitution (logistic regression, linear SVC, random forest) yields non-significant McNemar differences in 31/36 comparisons (Table~\ref{tab:mcnemar_obd_baselines}).
     
     \paragraph{JSD decay and the discrimination boundary.}
     Token-level JSD between adjacent generations reveals two decay regimes (Figure~\ref{fig:jsd_decay}): C4 models exhibit a sharp cliff (0v1 JSD exceeds 1v2 by 6--56$\times$) followed by rapid convergence, while arXiv decays ${\sim}$3$\times$ slower ($\lambda \approx 1.0$ vs.\ $2.9$); cross-model consistency indicates the contraction rate is domain-governed.
     The retrain-chain ($\lambda = 0.285$, no cliff) reflects model-adaptation artifacts: $\Kempirical{\geq}4$ (Pythia-1.4B LoRA; Table~\ref{tab:retrain_ci}), far exceeding prompt-chain $\Kempirical{=}2$ (Appendix~\ref{sec:jsd_extended}).
     
     \paragraph{Graduated filtering failure.}
     For web text, 2v3 $\auc < \theta$ across all configurations, so depth assignments beyond $d{=}2$ approximate random labels and graduated weighting injects noise (Appendix~\ref{sec:extended_limitations}).
     The strategy ordering binary $>$ graduated aligns with $\Kempirical{=}2$; on arXiv ($\Kempirical{=}3$), graduated filtering similarly shows no improvement, consistent with $\Kempirical$ capturing discriminability rather than curation utility.
     
     \paragraph{Representation and scorer dependence.}
     $\Kempirical$ is representation-dependent (frozen Qwen embeddings yield $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 3$ vs.\ $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 2$ for Pythia and OLMo; Table~\ref{tab:neural_probe_crossmodel}) and scorer-dependent (cross-family scoring reduces Wikipedia $\Kempirical$ from 2 to 1, Appendix~\ref{sec:wiki_domain}, while preserving C4 feature rankings $\rho > 0.90$ and $\Kempirical$ for 6/8 configurations, Section~\ref{sec:transfer}).
     
     \paragraph{Domain dependence of $\Kempirical$.}
     C4 web text yields $\Kempirical{=}2$ (8/9 configurations, 1B--14B), arXiv yields $\Kempirical{=}3$ (five models, four families; Section~\ref{sec:cross_domain}), and Wikipedia yields $\Kempirical{=}1$ with scorer confound controlled (Appendix~\ref{sec:wiki_domain}), producing a consistent domain ordering that correlates with lexical diversity (Table~\ref{tab:domain-ttr-entropy}).
     The only C4 exception (Mistral-7B, $\Kempirical{=}1$) has $\Kempirical{=}3$ on arXiv, reinforcing the domain-governed interpretation (Appendix~\ref{sec:boundary_extended}); a LaTeX markup ablation confirms content-level properties dominate (2v3 $\Delta = -1.8$\,pp, $\Kempirical$ unchanged; Appendix~\ref{sec:latex_ablation}).
     
     \paragraph{Surprisal attenuation and boundary variation.}
     \label{sec:surprisal_attenuation}
     Feature-level analysis reveals that the Mistral-7B exception ($\Kempirical{=}1$) arises from compressed surprisal dynamic range (depth-0 mean surprisal 2.32 vs.\ 2.90--3.02 for $\Kempirical{=}2$ models; mean $|\text{Cohen's}\;d| = 0.112$, the weakest; Table~\ref{tab:cohens_d_full}).
     Across six C4 configurations, larger 0$\to$1 surprisal drops correlate with higher 1v2 $\auc$ (Table~\ref{tab:borderline_features}), because generators that better approximate the training distribution produce less per-step degradation; this attenuation mechanism predicts that $\Kempirical$ may decrease as capability scales beyond 14B (Qwen-14B still yields $\Kempirical{=}2$, $\auc = 0.621$).
     
     \paragraph{Why $\Kempirical$ matters.}
     For curation, $\Kempirical{=}2$ validates binary filtering for web text (Section~\ref{sec:filtering}) and provides post-hoc grounding for the binary approach of \citet{drayson2025}.
     ```

     ```latex
     We measure $\Kempirical{=}2$ for web text (8/9 configurations, 5 families, 1B--14B; $p \leq 0.002$) and $\Kempirical{=}3$ for arXiv (5 models); generator-independent features ($\rho > 0.90$) support binary filtering as the optimal curation granularity.
     ```

   - **`docs/paper/related_work.tex`** — All 5 placeholder citations replaced with real ones. Current content:
     ```latex
     \section{Related Work}
     \label{sec:related_work}
     
     \paragraph{Model collapse and recursive degradation.}
     Iterative training on model outputs causes tail-distribution degradation~\cite{shumailov2024collapse, alemohammad2024selfconsuming, dohmatob2024model, dohmatob2025strong}, diversity contraction~\cite{guo2024curious, padmakumar2024writing, briesch2023selfconsuming}, and predictable convergence~\cite{schaeffer2025position, kazdan2025collapse, gerstgrasser2024model, shi2025collapse}.
     \citet{gambetta2024surplexity} propose surplexity, a single surprisal-based collapse indicator; our 15-dimensional feature set captures richer distributional structure and enables cross-model transfer.
     \citet{amin2025escaping} show that weak-data curation can mitigate collapse, connecting to our filtering evaluation (Section~\ref{sec:filtering}).
     Our prompt-chain operates in the replace regime~\cite{kazdan2025collapse}; $\Kempirical = 2$ is therefore a conservative lower bound.
     This literature characterizes macro dynamics but provides no per-text depth estimation, the gap we address.
     
     \paragraph{Beyond binary detection.}
     Binary detectors~\cite{gehrmann2019gltr, mitchell2023detectgpt, bao2024fast, hans2024binoculars, basani2026diveye, zhang2024minkpp} achieve strong human-vs.-machine separation but produce no depth estimate; surveys~\cite{wu2023survey, tang2024science} consolidate this landscape but frame detection as binary.
     DivEye~\cite{basani2026diveye} shares 4 of our 15 features but applies them to binary detection; our extended set captures cross-generational degradation.
     Recent work extends beyond binary labels to collaboration degree~\cite{cheng2025beyond}, contamination hierarchies~\cite{mehta2025beyond}, and multigenerator benchmarks~\cite{wang2024semeval}; robustness concerns arise under paraphrasing~\cite{sadasivan2023detect, krishna2023paraphrasing} and cross-mode transfer~\cite{hao2025rewrite}; CoDeC~\cite{zawalski2026codec} addresses contamination via membership inference.
     Iterative paraphrasing progressively degrades detection accuracy~\cite{krishna2023paraphrasing}; our rewrite-mode chain quantifies the depth at which this saturation occurs via $\Kempirical$.
     None estimates recursive generation depth.
     Watermarking~\cite{kirchenbauer2023watermark} embeds detectable signals at generation time but does not apply to text already in the wild.
     
     \paragraph{Data selection and curation.}
     DSIR~\cite{xie2023dsir}, D4~\cite{tirumala2023d4}, DoReMi~\cite{xie2023doremi}, and FineWeb~\cite{penedo2024fineweb} address corpus quality through domain-aware filtering and deduplication but are depth-agnostic; Drayson et al.~\cite{drayson2025} combined binary detection with importance resampling, a special case when $\Kstar = 1$.
     \citet{oren2024proving} address contamination detection in pre-training data; our method extends this to depth-granular resolution.
     
     \paragraph{Ordinal classification and probing.}
     Our OBD classifier follows Frank \& Hall's~\cite{frank2001ordinal} binary decomposition; CORAL~\cite{cao2020coral} provides a rank-consistent neural alternative.
     Our neural probe extends probing~\cite{alain2017understanding, belinkov2022probing} to the discrimination boundary.
     ```

```json
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\\label{sec:why_kstar}\n\\label{sec:one_wash}\n\\label{sec:negative_graduated}\n\\label{sec:repr_dependence}\n\\label{sec:boundary_cases}\n\\label{sec:length_confound}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_jsd_decay.pdf}\n\\caption{Token-level JSD between adjacent depth pairs on log scale. Blue band: min-max range across 8 C4 models (1B--14B) with $\\Kempirical{=}2$; dashed purple: Mistral-7B ($\\Kempirical{=}1$, cross-scorer validated); green: arXiv Pythia ($\\Kempirical{=}3$); dotted red: retrain-chain ($\\Kempirical{\\geq}4$). C4 models exhibit a sharp cliff ($\\lambda \\approx 2.2$--$3.5$), while arXiv decays gradually ($\\lambda \\approx 1.0$) and retrain-chain shows no cliff ($\\lambda = 0.285$).}\n\\label{fig:jsd_decay}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{One-wash laundering (web text).}\nFor general web text, $\\Kempirical{=}2$ reflects concentrated information loss: 0v1 is near-trivially separable ($\\auc \\geq 0.92$), 1v2 drops by ${\\sim}$30\\,pp, and 2v3 falls below $\\theta$.\nA single generation pass renders text indistinguishable from multiply recycled content, limiting depth-aware curation to a binary boundary.\nConverging evidence supports a data-intrinsic interpretation: OBD achieves 96\\% depth-0 recall but only 14--22\\% for depths 2--4; frontier LLMs show 8--38\\% (McNemar $p > 0.05$ vs.\\ OBD; Table~\\ref{tab:s3_llm_zeroshot}).\nBaseline classifier substitution (logistic regression, linear SVC, random forest) yields non-significant McNemar differences in 31/36 comparisons (Table~\\ref{tab:mcnemar_obd_baselines}).\n\n\\paragraph{JSD decay and the discrimination boundary.}\nToken-level JSD between adjacent generations reveals two decay regimes (Figure~\\ref{fig:jsd_decay}): C4 models exhibit a sharp cliff (0v1 JSD exceeds 1v2 by 6--56$\\times$) followed by rapid convergence, while arXiv decays ${\\sim}$3$\\times$ slower ($\\lambda \\approx 1.0$ vs.\\ $2.9$); cross-model consistency indicates the contraction rate is domain-governed.\nThe retrain-chain ($\\lambda = 0.285$, no cliff) reflects model-adaptation artifacts: $\\Kempirical{\\geq}4$ (Pythia-1.4B LoRA; Table~\\ref{tab:retrain_ci}), far exceeding prompt-chain $\\Kempirical{=}2$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:jsd_extended}).\n\n\\paragraph{Graduated filtering failure.}\nFor web text, 2v3 $\\auc < \\theta$ across all configurations, so depth assignments beyond $d{=}2$ approximate random labels and graduated weighting injects noise (Appendix~\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}).\nThe strategy ordering binary $>$ graduated aligns with $\\Kempirical{=}2$; on arXiv ($\\Kempirical{=}3$), graduated filtering similarly shows no improvement, consistent with $\\Kempirical$ capturing discriminability rather than curation utility.\n\n\\paragraph{Representation and scorer dependence.}\n$\\Kempirical$ is representation-dependent (frozen Qwen embeddings yield $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}} = 3$ vs.\\ $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}} = 2$ for Pythia and OLMo; Table~\\ref{tab:neural_probe_crossmodel}) and scorer-dependent (cross-family scoring reduces Wikipedia $\\Kempirical$ from 2 to 1, Appendix~\\ref{sec:wiki_domain}, while preserving C4 feature rankings $\\rho > 0.90$ and $\\Kempirical$ for 6/8 configurations, Section~\\ref{sec:transfer}).\n\n\\paragraph{Domain dependence of $\\Kempirical$.}\nC4 web text yields $\\Kempirical{=}2$ (8/9 configurations, 1B--14B), arXiv yields $\\Kempirical{=}3$ (five models, four families; Section~\\ref{sec:cross_domain}), and Wikipedia yields $\\Kempirical{=}1$ with scorer confound controlled (Appendix~\\ref{sec:wiki_domain}), producing a consistent domain ordering that correlates with lexical diversity (Table~\\ref{tab:domain-ttr-entropy}).\nThe only C4 exception (Mistral-7B, $\\Kempirical{=}1$) has $\\Kempirical{=}3$ on arXiv, reinforcing the domain-governed interpretation (Appendix~\\ref{sec:boundary_extended}); a LaTeX markup ablation confirms content-level properties dominate (2v3 $\\Delta = -1.8$\\,pp, $\\Kempirical$ unchanged; Appendix~\\ref{sec:latex_ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Surprisal attenuation and boundary variation.}\n\\label{sec:surprisal_attenuation}\nFeature-level analysis reveals that the Mistral-7B exception ($\\Kempirical{=}1$) arises from compressed surprisal dynamic range (depth-0 mean surprisal 2.32 vs.\\ 2.90--3.02 for $\\Kempirical{=}2$ models; mean $|\\text{Cohen's}\\;d| = 0.112$, the weakest; Table~\\ref{tab:cohens_d_full}).\nAcross six C4 configurations, larger 0$\\to$1 surprisal drops correlate with higher 1v2 $\\auc$ (Table~\\ref{tab:borderline_features}), because generators that better approximate the training distribution produce less per-step degradation; this attenuation mechanism predicts that $\\Kempirical$ may decrease as capability scales beyond 14B (Qwen-14B still yields $\\Kempirical{=}2$, $\\auc = 0.621$).\n\n\\paragraph{Why $\\Kempirical$ matters.}\nFor curation, $\\Kempirical{=}2$ validates binary filtering for web text (Section~\\ref{sec:filtering}) and provides post-hoc grounding for the binary approach of \\citet{drayson2025}.\n",

        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 45
      }
    },
```

```json
    "type": "skill_listing",
    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",
```

### 入稿要求

1. **Section 4 / Experiments**：新增 code domain 结果段落。Code K*=2 与 C4 相同但 1v2 AUC 更高 (0.74 vs 0.61-0.70)。2v3 骤降至 chance (JSD 10x 衰减)。强调 4 域矩阵完整。

```bibtex
@article{husain2019codesearchnet,
  title={CodeSearchNet Challenge: Evaluating the State of Semantic Code Search},
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01JrhgoswSjJiwmvHTpvfiq7`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Web-scale text corpora are increasingly contaminated by synthetic content that has itself been generated from earlier synthetic content.
5	Estimates suggest that a substantial fraction of online text is machine-generated~\cite{sun2025aigt, spennemann2025quantifying}, and this share continues to rise.
6	When models train on the outputs of earlier models, the resulting multi-generational synthetic text degrades with each iteration: tail modes vanish, lexical diversity contracts, and surprisal distributions flatten~\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model}.
7	This degradation arises in both \emph{retrain-chains} (iteratively training on model outputs) and \emph{prompt-chains} (recursively generating from previous outputs without retraining); we study the latter, which isolates generation-level information loss from training dynamics (Appendix~\ref{sec:extended_limitations}).
8	Yet the dominant paradigm treats all machine-generated text identically, discarding the generational depth dimension.
9	
10	This gap cuts across multiple communities.
11	Binary detectors~\cite{basani2026diveye, zhang2024minkpp} produce no depth estimate (Section~\ref{sec:binary_confidence}).
12	Membership inference~\cite{zawalski2026codec} addresses a different question, and the model collapse literature~\cite{shumailov2024collapse, alemohammad2024selfconsuming, schaeffer2025position} provides no per-text tool.
13	Data selection methods~\cite{xie2023dsir, tirumala2023d4, drayson2025} likewise ignore recursive depth.
14	No existing method estimates how many rounds of recursive generation a text has undergone.
15	
16	Knowing whether the discriminable depth is finite, and where it saturates, has direct practical consequences.
17	For data curation, the saturation point determines filtering granularity: if discrimination collapses at depth 2, graduated weighting beyond that boundary injects noise, and binary filtering becomes the principled choice.
18	Model collapse theory predicts monotonic quality degradation across generations, but existing analyses are population-level and provide no per-text measure of where degradation saturates.
19	$\Kempirical$ fills this gap by localizing the generation at which adjacent outputs become empirically indistinguishable.
20	
21	The Data Processing Inequality implies that information degrades monotonically across generations, motivating a finite discrimination boundary $\Kstar$.
22	We empirically measure $\Kempirical$ (the maximum depth at which a classifier reliably distinguishes adjacent generations) using 15 statistical features computed via a fixed reference scorer (Section~\ref{sec:features}) and an Ordinal Binary Decomposition (OBD) LightGBM classifier.
23	We operationalize $\Kempirical$ as the deepest $k \geq 1$ where the pairwise $\auc_{k-1,k}$ exceeds $\theta = 0.60$ (Section~\ref{sec:formulation}).
24	
25	Our contributions:
26	\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),nosep]
27	  \item \textbf{First empirical measurement of $\Kempirical$} across 9 model configurations (5 architecture families, 1B--14B), 3 source domains, and 4 decoding strategies at $\theta{=}0.60$: 4/5 families (8/9 configurations) yield $\Kempirical{=}2$ on C4 ($p \leq 0.002$; Section~\ref{sec:kstar_boundary}).
28	  Both supervised OBD and zero-shot frontier Large Language Models (LLMs) show no statistically significant difference beyond $\Kempirical$ (McNemar $p > 0.05$, $N{=}500$; Section~\ref{sec:why_kstar}).
29	  \item \textbf{Domain ordering invariance}: the hierarchy arXiv ($\Kempirical{=}3$) $>$ C4 ($\Kempirical{=}2$) $>$ Wikipedia ($\Kempirical{=}1$) is preserved across six models spanning four architecture families, three scales (1B--14B), and four decoding strategies, with $\Kempirical$ consistently varying by source domain (Section~\ref{sec:cross_domain}).
30	  \item \textbf{Practical validation}: largely generator-independent features enable 80--92\% accuracy retention among tested continuation-mode configurations ($\rho > 0.90$; Section~\ref{sec:transfer}), and $\Kempirical{=}2$ correctly predicts binary filtering as optimal granularity for web text (Section~\ref{sec:filtering}).
31	\end{enumerate}
32	
```

```text
29	\title{Generational Depth Estimation of Recursive Synthetic Text:\\Measuring the Discrimination Boundary}
30	
31	\author{Anonymous}
32	
33	\begin{document}
34	\maketitle
35	
36	% === Abstract ===
37	\begin{abstract}
38	Web-scale corpora are increasingly contaminated by recursive synthetic content, yet no method estimates a document's generational depth.
39	We measure the discrimination boundary $\Kempirical$ (the maximum depth at which adjacent generations remain distinguishable) using 15 statistical features and an OBD-LightGBM classifier.
40	Across five architecture families (1B--14B), three source domains, and four decoding strategies at $\theta{=}0.60$ (AUC decision threshold), $\Kempirical{=}2$ for general web text (8/9 model configurations) and $\Kempirical{=}3$ for structured academic text (arXiv).
41	A single additional generation pass suffices to render first-generation synthetic web text indistinguishable from deeper synthetic content, a \emph{one-wash laundering} effect.
42	Largely generator-independent statistical features, computed via a fixed reference scorer, enable 80--92\% accuracy retention among continuation-mode generators, and cross-scorer validation confirms that $\Kempirical$ is largely robust to scorer choice.
43	Downstream, $\Kempirical$ characterizes discriminability boundaries that inform, but do not directly determine, filtering strategy; for web text ($\Kempirical{=}2$), binary filtering aligns with the two-level discriminability structure.
44	\end{abstract}
45	
46	% === Section 1: Introduction ===
47	\input{introduction}
48	
49	% === Section 2: Related Work ===
50	\input{related_work}
51	
52	% === Section 3: Method ===
53	\input{method}
54	
55	% === Section 4: Experiments ===
56	\input{experiments_c1}
57	
58	% === Section 5: K*-Aware Data Filtering ===
59	\input{experiments_c3}
60	
61	% === Section 6: Discussion ===
62	\input{discussion}
63	
64	% === Section 7: Conclusion ===
65	\input{conclusion}

67	% === Limitations (ACL required, unnumbered) ===
68	\clearpage
69	\section*{Limitations}
70	
71	Our findings are bounded by:
72	(1)~\emph{Scope}: model scale $\leq$14B (5 architecture families), English text (C4, Wikipedia, arXiv), four decoding strategies; arXiv $\Kempirical{=}3$ is confirmed with five models across four architecture families but with reduced sample size ($n{=}2{,}200$ per model); subsampling analysis indicates $n{\geq}2{,}500$ suffices for 100\% reliable $\Kempirical$ determination (Appendix~\ref{sec:power_curve}).
73	(2)~\emph{Protocol}: all main experiments use prompt-chain, which isolates text-intrinsic degradation from training dynamics; real-world contamination involves both prompting and retraining, and retrain-chain yields $\Kempirical{\geq}4$ (Pythia-1.4B LoRA; Appendix~\ref{sec:extended_limitations}), so prompt-chain $\Kempirical$ may underestimate discriminability in mixed settings.
74	(3)~\emph{Threshold}: $\Kempirical$ ranges from 2--3 at $\theta{=}0.55$ to 1 at $\theta{=}0.70$; 8/9 configurations yield $\Kempirical{=}2$ at $\theta{=}0.60$ (Mistral-7B: $\Kempirical{=}1$).
75	(4)~\emph{Marginal boundary}: Qwen-continuation's 1v2 CI lower bound exceeds $\theta$ by only 0.002; cross-scorer validation drops this to $\Kempirical{=}1$ (Section~\ref{sec:transfer}). LLaMA-3.1-8B represents the most marginal $\Kempirical{=}2$ assignment, with the 1v2 CI lower bound within 0.3 percentage points of $\theta$.
76	(5)~\emph{Feature set}: the 15D features yield a feature-set-specific lower bound ($\Ktheory$ may be strictly higher); neural probe $\Kempirical$ is model-dependent ($3$ for Qwen, $2$ for Pythia/OLMo).
77	(6)~\emph{Segment length}: $\Kempirical{=}2$ confirmed for 128 and 256 tokens (CI lower $> \theta$); 512 tokens yields point estimate $\Kempirical{=}2$ but CI lower marginally below $\theta$ ($0.5979$), indicating the boundary weakens at longer generation lengths.
78	(7)~\emph{Curation applicability}: the filtering evaluation is validated on two architectures (Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct and Pythia-1.4B) with consistent strategy ordering (Appendix~\ref{sec:pythia_filtering}), though absolute improvement remains small and effect sizes diminish at lower contamination ratios (10--50\%; Appendix~\ref{sec:dose_response}).
79	(8)~\emph{Family balance}: the Qwen2.5 family provides 5/9 configurations; though spanning three scales (1.5B, 7B, 14B) and two training variants (base, instruct), aggregate statistics weight this family more heavily than the four single-configuration families.
80	Additional limitations (cross-model scoring sensitivity, feature ranking variability at the 1v2 boundary, structural composition confound analysis) appear in Appendix~\ref{sec:extended_limitations}.
81	
82	% === Ethics Statement (ACL required, unnumbered) ===
83	\section*{Ethics Statement}
84	
85	Generational depth estimation is intended for data curation and corpus auditing, positive-sum applications that improve training data quality.
86	Our experiments use publicly available corpora (C4, Wikipedia, arXiv abstracts) and do not involve personally identifiable information.
87	A potential dual-use concern is that depth estimation techniques could inform adversarial strategies to evade detection (e.g., by calibrating rewriting to mimic earlier-generation statistics); we note that this risk is shared by all detection research and that the binary detection boundary ($\auc \geq 0.96$ at 0v1) remains robust.
88	Code and generated corpora will be released upon acceptance to enable reproducibility.
89	
90	\bibliography{references}
91	
92	\input{appendix}
93	\input{appendix_extended}
94	
95	\end{document}
```

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We evaluate generational depth estimation on nine model configurations spanning five architecture families:
5	Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct (rewrite and continuation), Qwen2.5-1.5B (base), Qwen2.5-7B, Qwen2.5-14B, LLaMA-3.1-8B, and Mistral-7B, all in continuation mode except where noted.
6	All experiments use the OBD-LightGBM classifier with the 15-dimensional feature set (Section~\ref{sec:method}), with 5{,}000 texts per depth level (generations 0--5).
7	
8	%% ====================================================================
9	\subsection{\texorpdfstring{$\Kempirical \leq 2$}{K*<=2} Across Model Configurations (C4)}
10	\label{sec:kstar_boundary}
11	
12	\begin{table*}[t]
13	\centering
14	\small
15	\caption{Pairwise $\auc$ for adjacent depth pairs with bootstrap 95\% CIs and permutation $p$-values for boundary rows.
16	$\Kempirical = 2$ requires 1v2 $\auc > \theta{=}0.60$ and 2v3 $\auc < \theta$; CIs quantify boundary uncertainty.
17	Extended results (all models) in Table~\ref{tab:kstar_ci_extended}.}
18	\label{tab:kstar_ci}
19	\begin{tabular}{lcccccccccc}
20	\toprule
21	 & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Qwen-rw} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Pythia} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{OLMo} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Qwen-cont} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Qwen-7B\rlap{$^\ddagger$}} \\
22	\cmidrule(lr){2-3}\cmidrule(lr){4-5}\cmidrule(lr){6-7}\cmidrule(lr){8-9}\cmidrule(lr){10-11}
23	 & $\auc$ & 95\% CI & $\auc$ & 95\% CI & $\auc$ & 95\% CI & $\auc$ & 95\% CI & $\auc$ & 95\% CI \\
24	\midrule
25	0v1 & .9956 & & .9932 & & .9970 & & .9971 & & .9638 & [.961,\,.967] \\
26	1v2 & .6955 & [.686,\,.704]\rlap{$^*$} & .6539 & [.645,\,.663]\rlap{$^*$} & .6145 & [.605,\,.625]\rlap{$^*$} & .6112 & [.602,\,.621]\rlap{$^*$} & .6168 & [.608,\,.626]\rlap{$^*$} \\
27	2v3 & .5893 & [.580,\,.599] & .5514 & [.542,\,.563] & .5496 & [.540,\,.560] & .5609 & [.551,\,.571] & .5492 & [.539,\,.559] \\
28	3v4 & .5435 & & .5152 & & .5091 & & .5409 & & .5276 & \\
29	4v5 & .5340 & & .5136 & & .5045 & & .5141 & & .5085 & \\
30	\bottomrule
31	\multicolumn{11}{l}{\footnotesize $^*$Permutation $p < 0.001$ for all 1v2 comparisons shown.} \\
32	\multicolumn{11}{l}{\footnotesize $^\ddagger$Generator: Qwen2.5-7B (fp16); scorer: Qwen2.5-1.5B.}
33	\end{tabular}
34	\end{table*}
35	
36	\begin{figure}[t]
37	\centering
38	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_auc_decay.pdf}
39	\caption{Pairwise $\auc$ decay across depth pairs for model-mode configurations (solid) and decoding strategies (dashed). The horizontal dashed line marks $\theta = 0.60$.}
40	\label{fig:auc_decay}
41	\end{figure}
42	
43	Table~\ref{tab:kstar_ci} and Figure~\ref{fig:auc_decay} show $\Kempirical \leq 2$ across all 9 model configurations: 8/9 yield $\Kempirical{=}2$ (1v2 $\auc$ range: 0.6112--0.6955; all CI lower bounds $> \theta$; permutation $p < 0.001$), while Mistral-7B is the only exception ($\Kempirical{=}1$; 1v2 $\auc = 0.594$, below $\theta$; Section~\ref{sec:boundary_cases}).
44	LLaMA-3.1-8B yields $\Kempirical{=}2$ (0v1 $\auc = 0.9225$, 1v2 $\auc = 0.6123$, 95\% CI $[0.603, 0.622]$; borderline: 1/5 folds falls below $\theta$; Table~\ref{tab:kstar_ci_extended}).
45	All four decoding strategies likewise yield $\Kempirical{=}2$ (Table~\ref{tab:s2_decoding}).
46	The 1v2 spread due to generation mode ($\Delta = 8.4$\,pp within Qwen) exceeds the spread across models in continuation mode ($\Delta = 3.9$\,pp).
47	
48	\paragraph{Scale and instruction-tuning invariance.}
49	$\Kempirical{=}2$ persists from 1.5B through 14B: Qwen-14B (1v2 $\auc = 0.621$ $[0.612, 0.630]$), Qwen-7B ($0.6168$ $[0.608, 0.626]$), and LLaMA-3.1-8B ($0.6123$) all exceed $\theta$, despite progressively reduced 0v1 signal (0.960 at 14B, 0.964 at 7B, 0.923 at 8B vs.\ $> 0.99$ at $\leq$1.5B).
50	A base-vs.-instruct ablation at 1.5B likewise yields $\Kempirical = 2$ (1v2 $\auc = 0.626$); the boundary is not an instruction-tuning artifact.
51	
52	\begin{figure}[t]
53	\centering
54	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_kstar_theta.pdf}
55	\caption{$\Kempirical(\theta)$ sensitivity curve for six configurations. $\Kempirical$: maximum discriminable depth; $\theta$: AUC decision threshold. All configurations yield $\Kempirical{=}2$ at $\theta{=}0.60$ (dashed line). At $\theta{=}0.70$, all drop to $\Kempirical{=}1$.}
56	\label{fig:kstar_theta}
57	\end{figure}
58	
59	Figure~\ref{fig:kstar_theta} shows $\Kempirical$ as a function of $\theta$: web text $\Kempirical{=}2$ is stable across a 5\,pp window ($\theta \in [0.575, 0.625]$), with a gradual transition to $\Kempirical{=}3$ at $\theta{=}0.55$ (two Qwen configurations) and $\Kempirical{=}1$ at $\theta{=}0.70$.
60	The domain ordering (arXiv $>$ web $\geq$ cross-scorer) is preserved across the full $\theta$ range (Table~\ref{tab:theta_sensitivity}; Figure~\ref{fig:theta_sensitivity} in Appendix shows the complete model $\times$ domain $\theta$ sensitivity).
61	All four decoding strategies likewise yield $\Kempirical = 2$ at $\theta{=}0.60$ (1v2 $\auc \in [0.687, 0.704]$; Table~\ref{tab:s2_decoding}).
62	
63	%% ====================================================================
64	\subsection{Largely Generator-Independent Transfer}
65	\label{sec:transfer}
66	
67	\begin{figure}[t]
68	\centering
69	\includegraphics[width=0.85\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_transfer_heatmap.pdf}
70	\caption{Cross-model transfer heatmap. Each cell shows 6-class ordinal accuracy when trained on the row model and tested on the column model, divided by in-domain accuracy (retention rate). Pythia$\leftrightarrow$OLMo: 90--92\%; Qwen-rewrite$\to$continuation: 61--63\%.}
71	\label{fig:transfer_heatmap}
72	\end{figure}
73	
74	Continuation-mode classifiers transfer well across generators (Figure~\ref{fig:transfer_heatmap}; full matrix in Table~\ref{tab:s1_transfer}): Pythia$\leftrightarrow$OLMo retains 90--92\%, Qwen-cont$\leftrightarrow$Pythia 84--85\%, Qwen-cont$\leftrightarrow$OLMo 80--85\% (0v1 cross-model $\auc > 0.96$ for all pairs).
75	Qwen-rewrite classifiers transfer poorly (61--63\%), consistent with reliance on mode-specific artifacts rather than generalizable depth features.
76	
77	\paragraph{Cross-scorer validation.}
78	Replacing the self-scorer with Pythia-1.4B or OLMo-1B preserves feature rankings ($\rho > 0.90$; Table~\ref{tab:crossscorer}).
79	$\Kempirical$ is largely robust to scorer choice: across 22 cross-scorer configurations on C4 and arXiv, $\Kempirical$ was preserved in 20 cases; the two exceptions (Qwen-cont scored by Pythia on C4, Qwen-7B cross-scored on arXiv) are borderline, with AUC within 0.002 of $\theta$.
80	Qwen-cont is the most scorer-sensitive continuation-mode configuration: its self-scored 1v2 CI lower bound exceeds $\theta$ by only 0.2\,pp (Table~\ref{tab:kstar_ci}), and cross-family scoring pushes it below $\theta$.
81	For arXiv, cross-scoring with Pythia-1.4B preserves $\Kempirical{=}3$ for three of four generators (Qwen-1.5B, LLaMA-8B, Mistral-7B; 2v3 $\auc$ range: 0.608--0.632).
82	Qwen-7B exhibits a borderline decrease to $\Kempirical{=}2$ (2v3 $\auc = 0.599$ vs.\ $0.626$ self-scored, $\Delta = -2.7$\,pp; 3/5 folds above $\theta$), consistent with the Qwen family's scorer sensitivity observed on C4 and Wikipedia.
83	Bootstrap CIs (10{,}000 resamples) confirm $\Kempirical{=}3$ for Qwen-1.5B (CI lower $= 0.617$) and LLaMA-8B (CI lower $= 0.605$); for Mistral-7B (CI: $[0.593, 0.622]$) and Qwen-7B (CI: $[0.584, 0.613]$), the CI straddles $\theta$; their $\Kempirical{=}3$ assignment is sensitive to resampling variance (Table~\ref{tab:arxiv_crossscorer_ci}).
84	A three-way ANOVA (Generator $\times$ Scorer $\times$ Depth-Pair; $N{=}300$; Table~\ref{tab:anova_crossscorer}) confirms that Depth-Pair dominates variance ($\eta^2_p = 0.998$); Scorer is statistically significant ($p < .001$) but practically minor ($\eta^2_p = 0.192$), consistent with a detectable but small scorer influence on absolute discriminability.
85	
86	%% ====================================================================
87	\subsection{Feature Ordering and Generation Mode}
88	\label{sec:feature_degradation}
89	\label{sec:generation_mode}
90	
91	Among continuation-mode models, single-feature $\auc$ rankings are highly correlated ($\rho \geq 0.85$; Table~\ref{tab:top_features}).
92	The top-3 features (\texttt{surp\_d1\_std}, \texttt{surp\_std}, \texttt{surp\_d2\_std}) are shared across all three families: autoregressive generation progressively damps surprisal variability; however, no single feature subset of size ${<}8$ reliably sustains $\Kempirical{=}2$ (Appendix~\ref{sec:feature_subset}).
93	A feature group ablation (Appendix~\ref{sec:feature_ablation}) confirms that surprisal (base) dominates on arXiv (single AUC $= 0.809$, LOO $\Delta = 0.031$); on C4 and Wikipedia, all LOO drops fall within fold variance ($\leq 0.007$).
94	Qwen-rewrite diverges (\texttt{freq\_entropy} dominates), consistent with its poor cross-model transfer (61--63\%).
95	Within Qwen, rewrite yields 1v2 $\auc = 0.6955$ vs.\ continuation's $0.6112$ ($\Delta = +8.4$\,pp); continuation mode is therefore recommended as the canonical condition.
96	
97	%% ====================================================================
98	\subsection{Neural Probe}
99	\label{sec:neural_probe}
100	
101	Replacing the 15D features with frozen encoder hidden states (MLP probe, 5{,}000 texts per depth; Table~\ref{tab:neural_probe_crossmodel}), Qwen achieves $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 3$ (2v3 $\auc = 0.6159$), while Pythia and OLMo remain at $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}} = 2$ ($\auc_{2v3}$: 0.5963, 0.5892).
102	The Qwen-only elevation reflects self-familiarity artifacts that transfer poorly (Section~\ref{sec:transfer}); the 15D features yield consistent $\Kempirical = 2$ across all families.
103	
104	\noindent\begin{minipage}{\columnwidth}
105	\centering
106	\footnotesize
107	\captionof{table}{Neural probe pairwise AUC using frozen-encoder MLP. Each cell reports AUC for classifying depth $d$ vs.\ $d{+}1$ text. $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}}$: maximum depth where AUC exceeds $\theta = 0.60$.}
108	\label{tab:neural_probe_crossmodel}
109	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
110	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
111	\toprule
112	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{2v3 $\auc$} & \textbf{95\% CI} & $\Kempirical^{\text{neural}}$ \\
113	\midrule
114	Qwen-1.5B  & 0.6159 & $[0.610,\,0.622]$ & 3 \\
115	Pythia-1.4B & 0.5963 & $[0.589,\,0.603]$ & 2 \\
116	OLMo-1B    & 0.5892 & $[0.582,\,0.597]$ & 2 \\
117	\bottomrule
118	\end{tabular}}
119	\end{minipage}
120	
121	%% ====================================================================
122	\subsection{Cross-Domain Variation}
123	\label{sec:cross_domain}
124	
125	The preceding experiments use C4 (general web text).
126	To test whether $\Kempirical$ reflects domain structure or model identity, we evaluate on arXiv abstracts ($n{=}2{,}200$ per model) and Wikipedia ($n{=}5{,}000$ per model) across six model configurations spanning four architecture families (Table~\ref{tab:domain_kstar}).
127	
128	\paragraph{arXiv ($\Kempirical{=}3$): five-model cross-validation.}
129	All five domain-competent models yield $\Kempirical{=}3$ on arXiv, with boundary-pair $\auc$ (2v3) ranging from 0.6215 to 0.6545, all exceeding $\theta$.
130	The elevated signal reflects higher per-generation distributional shift (1v2 JSD $= 0.077$ vs.\ ${\sim}0.01$ for C4): formulaic features (LaTeX formatting, citation patterns, mathematical notation) degrade slowly under autoregressive generation and supply persistent depth signal across four architecture families and 1.4B--8B parameters.
131	Removing LaTeX markup reduces 2v3 AUC by ${\sim}$1.8\,pp but preserves $\Kempirical{=}3$ (CI lower $= 0.610 > \theta$; Table~\ref{tab:latex_ablation}); content-level properties dominate.
132	OLMo-1B constitutes a capacity failure rather than a counter-example; its arXiv generations are entirely out-of-domain (0v1 JSD $= 0.33$, near-total information loss at the first step), yielding $\Kempirical{=}1$.
133	Mistral-7B provides the strongest domain-dependence evidence: $\Kempirical{=}1$ on C4 (Section~\ref{sec:boundary_cases}) but $\Kempirical{=}3$ on arXiv: domain structure alone can shift $\Kempirical$ by two levels within the same model.
134	
135	\paragraph{Wikipedia ($\Kempirical{=}1$): single-generation saturation.}
136	With scorer confound controlled (Pythia-1.4B for Qwen generators; Qwen-1.5B, itself cross-family, for all others), all six models yield $\Kempirical{=}1$ on Wikipedia: 1v2 $\auc$ ranges from 0.499 (OLMo) to 0.595 (LLaMA), all below $\theta$.
137	Wikipedia represents the simplest domain tested; its encyclopedic regularity provides minimal structural signal beyond the 0v1 boundary, and a single generation pass suffices for complete saturation.
138	A same-family scorer confound inflates the apparent $\Kempirical$ for Qwen generators: Qwen-1.5B scoring yields $\Kempirical{=}2$ (1v2 $\auc \approx 0.61$--$0.62$), but cross-family scoring reduces 1v2 by 2.4\,pp to below $\theta$ (Appendix~\ref{sec:wiki_domain}).
139	This +1 depth bias under same-family scoring is specific to Qwen generators on Wikipedia and does not affect C4 results (Section~\ref{sec:transfer}).
140	
141	\paragraph{Domain as the primary factor.}
142	With the scorer confound controlled, the domain ordering is uniform across all tested models: arXiv ($\Kempirical{=}3$) $>$ C4 ($\Kempirical{=}2$) $>$ Wikipedia ($\Kempirical{=}1$).
143	Neither model scale (1B--14B), architecture family, nor decoding strategy alters this ordering; source-domain lexical diversity is the primary determinant of $\Kempirical$.
144	
145	\noindent\begin{minipage}{\columnwidth}
146	\centering
147	\footnotesize
148	\captionof{table}{Domain $\times$ Model $\Kempirical$ matrix (scorer-controlled). Wikipedia values use cross-family scorer (Pythia-1.4B). $^*$OLMo arXiv: capacity failure (out-of-domain generation). $^\dagger$Raw same-family scoring yields $\Kempirical{=}2$; controlled value shown (Appendix~\ref{sec:wiki_domain}).}
149	\label{tab:domain_kstar}
150	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
151	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
152	\toprule
153	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{C4} & \textbf{arXiv} & \textbf{Wikipedia} \\
154	\midrule
155	Qwen-1.5B    & 2 & 3 & 1$^\dagger$ \\
156	Qwen-7B      & 2 & 3 & 1$^\dagger$ \\
157	Pythia-1.4B  & 2 & 3 & 1 \\
158	OLMo-1B      & 2 & 1$^*$ & 1 \\
159	LLaMA-3.1-8B & 2 & 3 & 1 \\
160	Mistral-7B   & 1 & 3 & 1 \\
161	\bottomrule
162	\end{tabular}}
163	\end{minipage}
164	
165	%% ====================================================================
166	\subsection{Binary Detector Confidence}
167	\label{sec:binary_confidence}
168	
169	Binary detectors (DivEye~\cite{basani2026diveye}, Binoculars~\cite{hans2024binoculars}) show moderate rank correlations with depth ($|\rho| = 0.06$--$0.46$; Table~\ref{tab:s9_binary_spearman}) but fail as depth proxies: 6-class accuracy is at or below chance ($\leq 0.199$ vs.\ 0.167 baseline), pairwise $\auc$ yields $\Kempirical^{\text{binary}} \leq 1$, and multiple conditions exhibit non-monotonic AUC at deeper pairs.
170	The observed correlation is an artifact of 0v1 separation rather than fine-grained depth signal.
171	
```

its encyclopedic regularity provides minimal structural signal beyond the 0v1 boundary, and a single generation pass suffices for complete saturation.\nA same-family scorer confound inflates the apparent $\\Kempirical$ for Qwen generators: Qwen-1.5B scoring yields $\\Kempirical{=}2$ (1v2 $\\auc \\approx 0.61$--$0.62$), but cross-family scoring reduces 1v2 by 2.4\\,pp to below $\\theta$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:wiki_domain}).\nThis +1 depth bias under same-family scoring is specific to Qwen generators on Wikipedia and does not affect C4 results (Section~\\ref{sec:transfer}).\n\n\\paragraph{Domain as the primary factor.}\nWith the scorer confound controlled, the domain ordering is uniform across all tested models: arXiv ($\\Kempirical{=}3$) $>$ C4 ($\\Kempirical{=}2$) $>$ Wikipedia ($\\Kempirical{=}1$).\nNeither model scale (1B--14B), architecture family, nor decoding strategy alters this ordering; source-domain lexical diversity is the primary determinant of $\\Kempirical$.\n\n\\noindent\\begin{minipage}{\\columnwidth}\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\captionof{table}{Domain $\\times$ Model $\\Kempirical$ matrix (scorer-controlled). Wikipedia values use cross-family scorer (Pythia-1.4B). $^*$OLMo arXiv: capacity failure (out-of-domain generation). $^\\dagger$Raw same-family scoring yields $\\Kempirical{=}2$; controlled value shown (Appendix~\\ref{sec:wiki_domain}).}\n\\label{tab:domain_kstar}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{C4} & \\textbf{arXiv} & \\textbf{Wikipedia} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen-1.5B    & 2 & 3 & 1$^\\dagger$ \\\\\nQwen-7B      & 2 & 3 & 1$^\\dagger$ \\\\\nPythia-1.4B  & 2 & 3 & 1 \\\\\nOLMo-1B      & 2 & 1$^*$ & 1 \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & 2 & 3 & 1 \\\\\nMistral-7B   & 1 & 3 & 1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{minipage}\n\n%% ====================================================================\n\\subsection{Binary Detector Confidence}\n\\label{sec:binary_confidence}\n\nBinary detectors (DivEye~\\cite{basani2026diveye}, Binoculars~\\cite{hans2024binoculars}) show moderate rank correlations with depth ($|\\rho| = 0.06$--$0.46$; Table~\\ref{tab:s9_binary_spearman}) but fail as depth proxies: 6-class accuracy is at or below chance ($\\leq 0.199$ vs.\\ 0.167 baseline), pairwise $\\auc$ yields $\\Kempirical^{\\text{binary}} \\leq 1$, and multiple conditions exhibit non-monotonic AUC at deeper pairs.\nThe observed correlation is an artifact of 0v1 separation rather than fine-grained depth signal.\n",

```text
1	\section{Downstream Filtering Validation}
2	\label{sec:filtering}
3	
4	All depth estimates in this paper use prompt-chain generation (Section~\ref{sec:introduction}); the filtering evaluation below serves as a consistency check of whether $\Kempirical{=}2$ correctly predicts optimal curation granularity under this protocol, using Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct as proof of concept.
5	$\Kempirical = 2$ predicts that binary curation should suffice, and that graduated performance should converge toward binary as the decay steepness increases.
6	We compare six strategies on 30{,}000 documents (depths 0--5): (i)~\textbf{no-filter}, (ii)~\textbf{binary-ours} ($w = 1 - P(\text{synthetic})$ from 15D LightGBM), (iii)~\textbf{binary-Drayson}~\citep{drayson2025}, (iv)~\textbf{graduated} ($w = \exp(-\alpha \cdot \hat{d})$, $\alpha \in \{0.1,\ldots,0.9\}$), (v)~\textbf{surprisal} (retain above median), and (vi)~\textbf{DSIR}~\citep{xie2023dsir}.
7	We fine-tune Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct via LoRA (rank~8, $\alpha_{\text{LoRA}}{=}16$) for 3 epochs on MMLU (a multitask language understanding benchmark) and HellaSwag (a commonsense reasoning benchmark) across 5 seeds (51 total runs: 5 base strategies $\times$ 5 seeds $+$ 5 graduated-$\alpha$ values $\times$ 5 seeds $+$ 1 base-model reference; this 100\% synthetic setting is an upper bound~\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model}).
8	
9	\begin{table}[t]
10	\centering
11	\small
12	\caption{Downstream task performance (MMLU, HellaSwag) under six data-filtering strategies. Binary-ours uses the 15D LightGBM depth classifier; Graduated applies exponentially decaying weights $w = \exp(-\alpha \hat{d})$. Mean $\pm$ std over 5 seeds. Full $\alpha$ sweep in Appendix~\ref{sec:extended_limitations}.}
13	\label{tab:tab_filtering_main}
14	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{\input{tables/tab_filtering_main}}
15	\end{table}
16	
17	Binary-ours minimizes MMLU degradation most effectively (Table~\ref{tab:tab_filtering_main}), reducing the gap from $-$1.53\,pp (no-filter) to $-$0.77\,pp ($p_{\text{corr}} < 0.05$ vs.\ all alternatives).
18	The absolute improvement is modest (${\sim}$0.8\,pp), consistent with the constrained dynamic range of a 100\% synthetic upper-bound setting; real-world contamination ratios are typically lower and effect sizes scale accordingly.
19	The strategy ordering is interpretable through $\Kempirical$: binary filtering exploits the one reliable boundary (0v1), while graduated strategies assign weights to depths 2--5 based on near-random classifier outputs, injecting noise.
20	Graduated filtering converges monotonically toward binary: at $\alpha{=}0.9$, it matches binary-ours on HellaSwag exactly ($\Delta = 0$, $p_{\text{corr}} = 1.00$) but remains significantly worse on MMLU ($p_{\text{corr}} = 0.011$).
21	DSIR is indistinguishable from no-filter; depth-aware features are necessary for curation in recursively contaminated corpora (Figure~\ref{fig:filtering_strategies} in Appendix).
22	Binary filtering retains a consistent advantage across contamination levels (10--50\%), though effect sizes diminish 3--6$\times$ and synthetic data is net-beneficial at these ratios, so depth-based filtering may not be necessary in low-contamination regimes (Appendix~\ref{sec:dose_response}); graduated weighting remains indistinguishable from no-filter at all tested ratios.
23	Cross-model replication on Pythia-1.4B confirms the binary $>$ graduated ordering (Appendix~\ref{sec:pythia_filtering}), consistent with $\Kempirical{=}2$ predicting binary filtering as optimal granularity.
24	
```

Bold values indicate the dominant mode per domain. $K^*$ is remarkably consistent within each domain: C4 yields $K^*{=}2$, arXiv yields $K^*{=}3$, and Wikipedia yields $K^*{=}1$, confirming that generative depth is domain-dependent and low-bounded.}\n\\label{tab:domain_model_kstar}\n{\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Size} & \\textbf{C4}~$K^*$ & \\textbf{arXiv}~$K^*$ & \\textbf{Wiki}~$K^*$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen2.5       & 1.5B & \\textbf{2} & \\textbf{3} & \\textbf{1}\\textsuperscript{$\\dagger$} \\\\\nQwen2.5       & 7B   & \\textbf{2} & \\textbf{3} & \\textbf{1}\\textsuperscript{$\\dagger$} \\\\\nPythia        & 1.4B & \\textbf{2} & \\textbf{3} & \\textbf{1} \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1    & 8B   & \\textbf{2} & \\textbf{3} & \\textbf{1} \\\\\nMistral       & 7B   & 1\\textsuperscript{$\\ddagger$} & \\textbf{3} & \\textbf{1} \\\\\nOLMo          & 1B   & \\textbf{2} & 1\\textsuperscript{$*$} & \\textbf{1} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textit{Dominant} & & \\textbf{2} & \\textbf{3} & \\textbf{1} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\n{\\footnotesize\n\\textsuperscript{$\\dagger$}Scorer artifact: Qwen-1.5B scorer yields $K^*{=}2$; Pythia scorer yields $K^*{=}1$ (see \\S\\ref{sec:crossscorer}).\\\\\n\\textsuperscript{$\\ddagger$}Domain-specific one-wash: AUC(1v2)${=}0.594{<}\\theta$; arXiv rescues to $K^*{=}3$. Cross-scorer validated: Pythia-1.4B also yields $K^*{=}1$ (AUC${=}0.570$).\\\\\n\\textsuperscript{$*$}Capacity failure: OLMo-1B generates out-of-domain text on arXiv.\n}\n\\end{table}\n",
```json
      "numLines": 29,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 29
    }
```

```text
270	\bottomrule
271	\end{tabular}}
272	\end{table}
273	
274	\subsection{JSD Decay Analysis}
275	\label{sec:jsd_extended}
276	
277	The token-level JSD between adjacent generations reveals two distinct decay regimes (Figure~\ref{fig:jsd_decay}).
278	For C4 web text, 0v1 JSD exceeds 1v2 by 6--56$\times$ across all 8 models (1B--14B) with $\Kempirical{=}2$ (median cliff ratio ${\sim}$15$\times$), and subsequent pairs converge to a noise floor of ${\sim}$0.003--0.005.
279	Fitting $\jsd(k, k{+}1) \propto e^{-\lambda k}$ yields $\lambda \approx 2.2$--$3.5$ for C4 (mean ${\approx}\,2.9$), consistent with rapid mixing of $G_\delta$ toward its stationary distribution.
280	These decay rates represent qualitative characterizations from 5-point curves rather than precise estimates.
281	The empirical decay rate $\lambda$ is consistent within each domain but varies across domains: C4 yields $\lambda \approx 2.9$, Wikipedia $\lambda \approx 3.1$ (excluding OLMo, whose $\lambda = 6.3$ reflects a capacity-limited collapse: 0v1 $\jsd = 0.169$ with near-complete information loss at the first step, making the exponential fit unreliable), and arXiv $\lambda \approx 1.0$.
282	This cross-model consistency indicates that the contraction rate of $G_\delta$ is governed primarily by the structure of the input domain rather than the model architecture.
283	ArXiv text decays ${\sim}$3$\times$ slower than C4 ($\lambda \approx 1.0$ vs.\ $2.9$), with a correspondingly higher convergence floor (${\sim}$0.007--0.009 vs.\ ${\sim}$0.003--0.004).
284	Formulaic structural features (LaTeX formatting, citation patterns, section templates) degrade more slowly under autoregressive generation and supply persistent depth-distinguishing signal, explaining the elevated $\Kempirical{=}3$.
285	The retrain-chain exhibits a fundamentally different profile: $\lambda = 0.285$ with a cliff ratio of only 1.31$\times$ and no sharp drop between adjacent pairs.
286	This gradual, sustained divergence reflects cumulative model-adaptation artifacts rather than text-intrinsic information loss, further supporting the distinction between prompt-chain and retrain-chain $\Kempirical$ (Appendix~\ref{sec:boundary_extended}).
287	
288	\subsection{Boundary Cases and Scale Effect}
289	\label{sec:boundary_extended}
290	
291	Of 9 tested model configurations on C4, 8 yield $\Kempirical{=}2$; Mistral-7B is the only sub-2 exception ($\Kempirical{=}1$; 1v2 $\auc = 0.594$, CI $[0.584, 0.603]$), validating $\theta{=}0.60$ as an effective discriminator (gap: $0.594$ vs.\ $0.611$--$0.696$).
292	Mistral's cross-domain profile reinforces the domain-governed interpretation: despite $\Kempirical{=}1$ on both C4 and Wikipedia, it yields $\Kempirical{=}3$ on arXiv (1v2 $\auc = 0.820$, 2v3 $= 0.628$), and this profile persists under cross-scorer validation (Pythia-1.4B scorer: C4 $\Kempirical{=}1$, arXiv $\Kempirical{=}3$ (2v3 $\auc = 0.608$, CI $[0.593, 0.622]$)), indicating that domain lexical diversity can rescue even web-text one-wash generators (Table~\ref{tab:domain_kstar}).
293	Larger models produce more human-like text, reducing 0v1 JSD: 0.16--0.29 at $\leq$1.5B, 0.077--0.13 at 7--14B.
294	However, Qwen-14B still yields $\Kempirical{=}2$ (1v2 $\auc = 0.621$, CI $[0.612, 0.630]$), with 0v1 $\auc = 0.960$ comparable to Qwen-7B ($0.964$), indicating that scale in the 1B--14B range does not shift the discrimination boundary.
295	
296	\paragraph{Sample-size control.}
297	As a sample-size control, subsampling C4 to $n{=}2{,}200$ (matching arXiv) yields $\Kempirical{=}1$ (1v2 $\auc = 0.601$, CI $[0.586, 0.616]$).
298	Reduced sample size widens confidence intervals and produces more conservative $\Kempirical$ estimates, confirming that arXiv's $\Kempirical{=}3$ at the same sample size reflects genuine domain signal rather than a statistical artifact of smaller training sets.
299	
300	\subsection{LaTeX Markup Ablation (arXiv)}
301	\label{sec:latex_ablation}
302	
303	To disentangle content-level depth signal from formatting artifacts on arXiv, we strip all LaTeX markup (commands, environments, inline math delimiters) and re-extract features from the plain-text residual (Qwen-1.5B continuation, $n{=}2{,}200$; Table~\ref{tab:latex_ablation}).
304	
305	\begin{table}[h]
306	\centering
307	\small
308	\caption{arXiv pairwise $\auc$ with and without LaTeX markup (Qwen-1.5B continuation). Removing formatting reduces 2v3 by ${\sim}$1.8\,pp but $\Kempirical$ remains at 3 (CI lower $> \theta$). LaTeX stripping fraction increases with depth (0.6\% at depth 0, 4.6\% at depth 5), indicating that deeper generations produce proportionally more formatting tokens.}
309	\label{tab:latex_ablation}
310	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
311	\begin{tabular}{lccr}
312	\toprule
313	\textbf{Pair} & \textbf{Original} & \textbf{Stripped} & $\Delta$ \\
314	\midrule
315	0v1 & 0.9946 & 0.9948 & $+$0.0002 \\
316	1v2 & 0.8580 & 0.8297 & $-$0.028 \\
317	2v3 & 0.6427 $[0.628,\,0.658]$ & 0.6248 $[0.610,\,0.640]$ & $-$0.018 \\
318	3v4 & 0.5437 & 0.5468 & $+$0.003 \\
319	\midrule
320	$\Kempirical$ & 3 & 3 & --- \\
321	\bottomrule
322	\end{tabular}}
323	\end{table}
324	
325	Content-level statistical properties (surprisal distribution, vocabulary diversity) account for the large majority of the depth signal; LaTeX formatting contributes a modest but measurable increment (${\sim}$1.8\,pp at the boundary pair).
326	The depth-correlated increase in LaTeX stripping fraction (depth 0: $-$0.6\%, depth 5: $-$4.6\%) indicates that autoregressive generation progressively introduces formatting tokens, consistent with mode-seeking behavior in structured text.
327	
328	\subsection{Subsampling Power Curve}
329	\label{sec:power_curve}
```

```text
328	\subsection{Subsampling Power Curve}
329	\label{sec:power_curve}
330	
331	To characterize the minimum sample size required for reliable $\Kempirical$ determination, we subsample C4 at $n \in \{1000, 2500, 5000\}$ and measure 1v2 AUC stability across 20 random draws (Qwen-1.5B continuation; Table~\ref{tab:power_curve}).
332	
333	\begin{table}[h]
334	\centering
335	\small
336	\caption{Subsampling power analysis for $\Kempirical{=}2$ determination (C4, Qwen-1.5B continuation, 20 random draws). Detection probability: fraction of draws where 1v2 $\auc > \theta{=}0.60$.}
337	\label{tab:power_curve}
338	\begin{tabular}{rccc}
339	\toprule
340	$n$ & \textbf{Mean 1v2 $\auc$} & \textbf{Std} & \textbf{Detection Prob.} \\
341	\midrule
342	1{,}000 & 0.608 & 0.012 & 90\% \\
343	2{,}500 & 0.618 & 0.006 & 100\% \\
344	5{,}000 & 0.629 & 0.000 & 100\% \\
345	\bottomrule
346	\end{tabular}
347	\end{table}
348	
349	$n{\geq}2{,}500$ suffices for 100\% reliable $\Kempirical{=}2$ determination; at $n{=}1{,}000$, detection probability is 90\% with increased variance (std $= 0.012$), indicating that even moderately sized corpora support robust boundary estimation.
350	
351	\subsection{Feature Group Ablation}
352	\label{sec:feature_ablation}
353	
354	To assess the relative contribution of each feature group to depth discrimination, we train a plain LightGBM classifier (\texttt{n\_estimators}$=$200, \texttt{max\_depth}$=$6; distinct from the OBD-LightGBM used in Section~\ref{sec:method}) on the 1\,vs.\,2 task using (a) each feature group alone (\emph{single}) and (b) the full 15D set minus one group (\emph{leave-one-out}).
355	Representative models per domain: LLaMA-3.1-8B (C4), Qwen-1.5B continuation (arXiv), and the domain ablation configuration (Wikipedia).
356	
357	\begin{figure}[h]
358	\centering
359	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_feature_ablation.pdf}
360	\caption{Feature group ablation heatmap for 1\,vs.\,2 classification AUC. \emph{Left}: single-group performance. \emph{Right}: leave-one-out performance. Surprisal (base) features are the dominant signal on arXiv (single AUC $= 0.809$) but provide only weak marginal signal on C4 and Wikipedia.}
361	\label{fig:feature_ablation}
362	\end{figure}
363	
364	\begin{table}[h]
365	\centering
366	\small
367	\caption{Feature group ablation for 1\,vs.\,2 classification (AUC). \textbf{Single}: using only features from one group. \textbf{Leave-one-out}: removing one group from the full 15D feature set. $\Delta$ denotes the AUC drop from the full model. Classifier: plain LightGBM (\texttt{n\_estimators}$=$200, \texttt{max\_depth}$=$6), distinct from the OBD-LightGBM pipeline; AUC values are not directly comparable to Table~\ref{tab:kstar_ci}.}
368	\label{tab:feature_ablation}
369	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%

372	& \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{Single Group}} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{Leave-One-Out ($\Delta$)}} \\
373	\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-7}
374	Feature Group & C4 & arXiv & Wiki & C4 & arXiv & Wiki \\

385	
386	Surprisal (base) features dominate on arXiv (single-group AUC $= 0.809$; LOO drop $= 0.031$, the largest across all domain--group combinations), consistent with the persistent surprisal-distribution signal in structured academic text.
387	On C4 and Wikipedia, all LOO drops are small ($\leq 0.007$, within cross-fold standard deviation), indicating that no single feature group is individually critical for the weaker low-$\Kempirical$ signal.
388	Removing surprisal derivatives increases AUC on C4 (+0.003) and Wikipedia (+0.004), suggesting partial redundancy with the base surprisal group in low-$\Kempirical$ domains.
389	
390	\subsection{Classifier Choice: McNemar Test}
391	\label{sec:mcnemar_baselines}
392	
393	To verify that depth discriminability is driven by the 15D feature representation rather than classifier choice, we compare OBD-LightGBM against three baseline classifiers (logistic regression, linear SVC, random forest) on the depth-1 vs.\ depth-2 binary classification task using McNemar's test with continuity correction on 5-fold GroupKFold out-of-fold predictions (Table~\ref{tab:mcnemar_obd_baselines}).
394	Of 36 comparisons, 31 are non-significant ($p \geq 0.05$), and all five significant results concentrate in two model $\times$ domain conditions.
395	For LLaMA-8B on C4, all three baselines significantly outperform OBD ($p \leq 0.001$), likely reflecting LightGBM overfitting on the relatively noisy 8B-generated features rather than a systematic classifier advantage.
396	For Qwen-1.5B on Wikipedia, OBD outperforms both linear classifiers ($p < 0.001$) but not random forest ($p = 0.655$).
```

```text
432	\subsection{Domain Lexical Statistics}
433	\label{sec:domain_lexical_stats}
434	
435	Table~\ref{tab:domain-ttr-entropy} reports type-token ratio (TTR) and vocabulary entropy across three domains and six depth levels.
436	The TTR ordering at depth 0 (arXiv $>$ C4 $>$ Wikipedia) aligns with the $\Kempirical$ ordering, with large effect sizes (Cohen's $d = 1.52$ for arXiv vs.\ Wikipedia).
437	Vocabulary entropy shows a partial reversal at depth 0: C4 ($H = 6.225$) falls below Wikipedia ($H = 6.408$), while arXiv remains highest ($H = 6.853$).
438	This reversal likely reflects C4's greater content heterogeneity (higher variance: $\sigma = 0.706$ vs.\ $0.284$), which inflates TTR but depresses mean entropy.
439	As depth increases, all domains converge, with arXiv exhibiting the steepest decline, consistent with its higher $\Kempirical$ and slower convergence to the stationary distribution.
440

470	
471	\subsection{Leave-One-Family-Out Threshold Validation}
472	\label{sec:theta_lofo}
473	
474	To validate $\theta{=}0.60$ without circular reasoning, we perform leave-one-family-out (LOFO) cross-validation: each fold removes one architecture family, derives $\hat{\theta}$ as the midpoint between $\min(\auc_{1v2})$ and $\max(\auc_{2v3})$ over the remaining families, then predicts $\Kempirical$ for the held-out models.
475	Table~\ref{tab:theta_lofo} reports results across all three domains.
476	
477	On C4, 8/9 held-out predictions agree with the original $\Kempirical$ assignment at $\theta{=}0.60$ (agreement: 89\%).
478	The single mismatch is Qwen-1.5B-RW (rewrite mode), whose 2v3 AUC of 0.5893 lies above the LOFO-derived $\hat{\theta}{=}0.5725$ but below the global $\theta{=}0.60$; this configuration is also the sole rewrite-mode model, making the discrepancy attributable to mode rather than threshold instability.
479	The derived $\hat{\theta}$ values span $[0.5725, 0.6004]$, and $\theta{=}0.60$ falls within this range.
480	
481	ArXiv corroborates with 6/6 agreement (100\%), though the wide $\hat{\theta}$ range $[0.5735, 0.7296]$ reflects OLMo-1B's near-chance performance ($\auc_{1v2}{=}0.503$), which inflates the gap midpoint when OLMo is included in the training fold.
482	
483	Wikipedia yields only 1/6 agreement (17\%).
484	This failure is expected: most models produce near-chance 1v2 AUC on Wikipedia ($0.499$--$0.619$), so the gap between $\min(\auc_{1v2})$ and $\max(\auc_{2v3})$ is narrow or inverted, invalidating the gap-based threshold derivation.
485	Wikipedia's $\Kempirical{=}1$ assignment is independently confirmed by the global $\theta{=}0.60$ criterion (Section~\ref{sec:cross_domain}); LOFO simply cannot validate this because the method requires a clear AUC discontinuity between boundary and non-boundary pairs.
486
```

```text
528	
529	\subsection{Borderline Feature Analysis}
530	\label{sec:borderline_analysis}
531	
532	To understand why Mistral-7B yields $\Kempirical{=}1$ while other C4 models yield $\Kempirical{=}2$, we compute per-feature Cohen's $d$ (depth-1 vs.\ depth-2) and LightGBM feature importances across six C4 configurations spanning the full 1v2 AUC range (0.594--0.884).
533	
534	Mistral-7B exhibits the lowest depth-0 mean surprisal (2.32 vs.\ 2.90--3.02 for other models), indicating that the scorer model already finds Mistral's output highly predictable.
535	This compressed baseline limits the dynamic range available for detecting inter-generational degradation: all 15 Cohen's $d$ values are weak ($\bar{|d|} = 0.112$; Table~\ref{tab:cohens_d_full}), and no single feature exceeds 13\% of total classifier importance.
536	The borderline models (LLaMA-8B, Pythia at $T{=}1.2$) show a similar pattern at slightly higher effect sizes.
537
```

```json
    "originalFile": "% EMNLP 2026 Main — ARR May 2026\n% Generational Depth Contamination\n\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{amssymb}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{enumitem}\n\\usepackage{subcaption}\n\\usepackage{pifont}\n\\newcommand{\\cmark}{\\ding{51}}\n\\newcommand{\\xmark}{\\ding{55}}\n\\usepackage[section]{placeins}\n\n\\newcommand{\\Kstar}{{K^*}}\n\\newcommand{\\Kempirical}{{K^*_{\\text{empirical}}}}\n\\newcommand{\\Ktheory}{{K^*_{\\text{theory}}}}\n\\newcommand{\\auc}{\\text{AUC}}\n\\newcommand{\\jsd}{\\text{JSD}}\n\\newcommand{\\tpm}{\\ensuremath{\\,\\pm\\,}}\n\n\\title{Generational Depth Estimation of Recursive Synthetic Text:\\\\Measuring the Discrimination Boundary}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n% === Abstract ===\n\\begin{abstract}\nWeb-scale corpora are increasingly contaminated by recursive synthetic content, yet no method estimates a document's generational depth.\nWe measure the discrimination boundary $\\Kempirical$ (the maximum depth at which adjacent generations remain distinguishable) using 15 statistical features and an OBD-LightGBM classifier.\nAcross five architecture families (1B--14B), three source domains, and four decoding strategies at $\\theta{=}0.60$ (AUC decision threshold), $\\Kempirical{=}2$ for general web text (8/9 model configurations) and $\\Kempirical{=}3$ for structured academic text (arXiv).\nA single additional generation pass suffices to render first-generation synthetic web text indistinguishable from deeper synthetic content, a \\emph{one-wash laundering} effect.\nLargely generator-independent statistical features, computed via a fixed reference scorer, enable 80--92\\% accuracy retention among continuation-mode generators, and cross-scorer validation confirms that $\\Kempirical$ is largely robust to scorer choice.\nDownstream, $\\Kempirical$ characterizes discriminability boundaries that inform, but do not directly determine, filtering strategy; for web text ($\\Kempirical{=}2$), binary filtering aligns with the two-level discriminability structure.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n% === Section 1: Introduction ===\n\\input{introduction}\n\n% === Section 2: Related Work ===\n\\input{related_work}\n\n% === Section 3: Method ===\n\\input{method}\n\n% === Section 4: Experiments ===\n\\input{experiments_c1}\n\n% === Section 5: K*-Aware Data Filtering ===\n\\input{experiments_c3}\n\n% === Section 6: Discussion ===\n\\input{discussion}\n\n% === Section 7: Conclusion ===\n\\input{conclusion}\n\n% === Limitations (ACL required, unnumbered) ===\n\\clearpage\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur findings are bounded by:\n(1)~\\emph{Scope}: model scale $\\leq$14B (5 architecture families), English text (C4, Wikipedia, arXiv), four decoding strategies; arXiv $\\Kempirical{=}3$ is confirmed with five models across four architecture families but with reduced sample size ($n{=}2{,}200$ per model); subsampling analysis indicates $n{\\geq}2{,}500$ suffices for 100\\% reliable $\\Kempirical$ determination (Appendix~\\ref{sec:power_curve}).\n(2)~\\emph{Protocol}: all main experiments use prompt-chain, which isolates text-intrinsic degradation from training dynamics; real-world contamination involves both prompting and retraining, and retrain-chain yields $\\Kempirical{\\geq}4$ (Pythia-1.4B LoRA; Appendix~\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}), so prompt-chain $\\Kempirical$ may underestimate discriminability in mixed settings.\n(3)~\\emph{Threshold}: $\\Kempirical$ ranges from 2--3 at $\\theta{=}0.55$ to 1 at $\\theta{=}0.70$; 8/9 configurations yield $\\Kempirical{=}2$ at $\\theta{=}0.60$ (Mistral-7B: $\\Kempirical{=}1$).\n(4)~\\emph{Marginal boundary}: Qwen-continuation's 1v2 CI lower bound exceeds $\\theta$ by only 0.002; cross-scorer validation drops this to $\\Kempirical{=}1$ (Section~\\ref{sec:transfer}). LLaMA-3.1-8B represents the most marginal $\\Kempirical{=}2$ assignment, with the 1v2 CI lower bound within 0.3 percentage points of $\\theta$.\n(5)~\\emph{Feature set}: the 15D features yield a feature-set-specific lower bound ($\\Ktheory$ may be strictly higher); neural probe $\\Kempirical$ is model-dependent ($3$ for Qwen, $2$ for Pythia/OLMo).\n(6)~\\emph{Segment length}: $\\Kempirical{=}2$ confirmed for 128 and 256 tokens (CI lower $> \\theta$); 512 tokens yields point estimate $\\Kempirical{=}2$ but CI lower marginally below $\\theta$ ($0.5979$), indicating the boundary weakens at longer generation lengths.\n(7)~\\emph{Curation applicability}: the filtering evaluation is validated on two architectures (Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct and Pythia-1.4B) with consistent strategy ordering (Appendix~\\ref{sec:pythia_filtering}), though absolute improvement remains small and effect sizes diminish at lower contamination ratios (10--50\\%; Appendix~\\ref{sec:dose_response}).\n(8)~\\emph{Family balance}: the Qwen2.5 family provides 5/9 configurations; though spanning three scales (1.5B, 7B, 14B) and two training variants (base, instruct), aggregate statistics weight this family more heavily than the four single-configuration families.\nAdditional limitations (cross-model scoring sensitivity, feature ranking variability at the 1v2 boundary, structural composition confound analysis) appear in Appendix~\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}.\n\n% === Ethics Statement (ACL required, unnumbered) ===\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nGenerational depth estimation is intended for data curation and corpus auditing, positive-sum applications that improve training data quality.\nOur experiments use publicly available corpora (C4, Wikipedia, arXiv abstracts) and do not involve personally identifiable information.\nA potential dual-use concern is that depth estimation techniques could inform adversarial strategies to evade detection (e.g., by calibrating rewriting to mimic earlier-generation statistics); we note that this risk is shared by all detection research and that the binary detection boundary ($\\auc \\geq 0.96$ at 0v1) remains robust.\nCode and generated corpora will be released upon acceptance to enable reproducibility.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\input{appendix}\n\\input{appendix_extended}\n\n\\end{document}\n",
```

```json
    "oldString": "  \\item \\textbf{First empirical measurement of $\\Kempirical$} across 9 model configurations (5 architecture families, 1B--14B), 3 source domains, and 4 decoding strategies at $\\theta{=}0.60$: 4/5 families (8/9 configurations) yield $\\Kempirical{=}2$ on C4 ($p \\leq 0.002$; Section~\\ref{sec:kstar_boundary}).",
    "newString": "  \\item \\textbf{First empirical measurement of $\\Kempirical$} across 9 model configurations (5 architecture families, 1B--14B), 4 source domains (3 text + 1 code), and 4 decoding strategies at $\\theta{=}0.60$: 4/5 families (8/9 configurations) yield $\\Kempirical{=}2$ on C4 ($p \\leq 0.002$; Section~\\ref{sec:kstar_boundary}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWeb-scale text corpora are increasingly contaminated by synthetic content that has itself been generated from earlier synthetic content.\nEstimates suggest that a substantial fraction of online text is machine-generated~\\cite{sun2025aigt, spennemann2025quantifying}, and this share continues to rise.\nWhen models train on the outputs of earlier models, the resulting multi-generational synthetic text degrades with each iteration: tail modes vanish, lexical diversity contracts, and surprisal distributions flatten~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model}.\nThis degradation arises in both \\emph{retrain-chains} (iteratively training on model outputs) and \\emph{prompt-chains} (recursively generating from previous outputs without retraining); we study the latter, which isolates generation-level information loss from training dynamics (Appendix~\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}).\nYet the dominant paradigm treats all machine-generated text identically, discarding the generational depth dimension.\n\nThis gap cuts across multiple communities.\nBinary detectors~\\cite{basani2026diveye, zhang2024minkpp} produce no depth estimate (Section~\\ref{sec:binary_confidence}).\nMembership inference~\\cite{zawalski2026codec} addresses a different question, and the model collapse literature~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse, alemohammad2024selfconsuming, schaeffer2025position} provides no per-text tool.\nData selection methods~\\cite{xie2023dsir, tirumala2023d4, drayson2025} likewise ignore recursive depth.\nNo existing method estimates how many rounds of recursive generation a text has undergone.\n\nKnowing whether the discriminable depth is finite, and where it saturates, has direct practical consequences.\nFor data curation, the saturation point determines filtering granularity: if discrimination collapses at depth 2, graduated weighting beyond that boundary injects noise, and binary filtering becomes the principled choice.\nModel collapse theory predicts monotonic quality degradation across generations, but existing analyses are population-level and provide no per-text measure of where degradation saturates.\n$\\Kempirical$ fills this gap by localizing the generation at which adjacent outputs become empirically indistinguishable.\n\nThe Data Processing Inequality implies that information degrades monotonically across generations, motivating a finite discrimination boundary $\\Kstar$.\nWe empirically measure $\\Kempirical$ (the maximum depth at which a classifier reliably distinguishes adjacent generations) using 15 statistical features computed via a fixed reference scorer (Section~\\ref{sec:features}) and an Ordinal Binary Decomposition (OBD) LightGBM classifier.\nWe operationalize $\\Kempirical$ as the deepest $k \\geq 1$ where the pairwise $\\auc_{k-1,k}$ exceeds $\\theta = 0.60$ (Section~\\ref{sec:formulation}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=(\\alph*),nosep]\n  \\item \\textbf{First empirical measurement of $\\Kempirical$} across 9 model configurations (5 architecture families, 1B--14B), 3 source domains, and 4 decoding strategies at $\\theta{=}0.60$: 4/5 families (8/9 configurations) yield $\\Kempirical{=}2$ on C4 ($p \\leq 0.002$; Section~\\ref{sec:kstar_boundary}).\n  Both supervised OBD and zero-shot frontier Large Language Models (LLMs) show no statistically significant difference beyond $\\Kempirical$ (McNemar $p > 0.05$, $N{=}500$; Section~\\ref{sec:why_kstar}).\n  \\item \\textbf{Domain ordering invariance}: the hierarchy arXiv ($\\Kempirical{=}3$) $>$ C4 ($\\Kempirical{=}2$) $>$ Wikipedia ($\\Kempirical{=}1$) is preserved across six models spanning four architecture families, three scales (1B--14B), and four decoding strategies, with $\\Kempirical$ consistently varying by source domain (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_domain}).\n  \\item \\textbf{Practical validation}: largely generator-independent features enable 80--92\\% accuracy retention among tested continuation-mode configurations ($\\rho > 0.90$; Section~\\ref{sec:transfer}), and $\\Kempirical{=}2$ correctly predicts binary filtering as optimal granularity for web text (Section~\\ref{sec:filtering}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 24,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 24,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " ",
```
